# Chromium

## Overview

Chromium (Cr, atomic number 24) in the food system is a split case. Trivalent chromium (Cr3+) has an ambiguous history as a putative essential trace element and is the species in most dietary supplements. Hexavalent chromium (Cr6+) is a carcinogen by inhalation and has documented toxicity by ingestion, including in drinking water; its relevance to food is principally via drinking-water used in food preparation, via meat processing with certain contact materials, and via supply-chain contamination. The wiki maintains the species distinction where the source permits, and flags ambiguity where a source reports total chromium without specifying.

This page covers chromium as element and the trivalent species (Cr-III) by default, plus chromium speciation discipline. The hexavalent species has a dedicated page at [[metals/chromium-hexavalent]] because Cr-VI is a priority heavy-metal analyte whose toxicology, occurrence, regulatory, and testing posture all differ sharply from Cr-III. Sources that report total chromium without speciating route here as total-chromium occurrence context and must not be silently substituted for Cr-VI.

[[sources/ufelle2021-metals-chapter|Ufelle & Barchowsky 2021]] supports the Cr(III)/Cr(VI) distinction at the toxicology level, including hexavalent chromium absorption through sulfate/phosphate transporters, corrosive injury, allergic contact dermatitis, acute renal injury after high-dose ingestion, and lung-cancer/genotoxicity framing for Cr(VI); see [[metals/chromium-hexavalent]] for the species-specific synthesis.

Regulatory limits track the species split. The enforceable thresholds that name hexavalent chromium specifically are California's drinking-water Maximum Contaminant Level of 10 µg/L ([[regulations/ca-swrcb-hexavalent-chromium-mcl]]) and California's Proposition 65 listing and safe-harbor levels ([[regulations/oehha-chromium-vi-prop65]]); both are covered on the [[metals/chromium-hexavalent]] page. Total-chromium drinking-water MCLs (US EPA 100 µg/L, California 50 µg/L) apply to chromium as element and do not separate the hexavalent fraction.

## Status

This page is a sourced stub for chromium as element and Cr-III. The Cr-VI species page [[metals/chromium-hexavalent]] carries the substantive synthesis for the carcinogenic species, including the speciation gap in the loaded corpus. Cr-III-specific expansion (essentiality debate, supplement use, agricultural uptake patterns) awaits dedicated corpus ingest. See [methodology](/methodology) for the current state of the project.

## Planned sections

Toxicology of Cr3+ specifically (Cr6+ lives on the species page), Cr-III essentiality status and supplement context, typical exposure routes for total chromium, food and water sources (linked to [ingredients](/ingredients), [products](/products), and [supply-chain](/supply-chain)), total-chromium regulatory frameworks (linked to [regulations](/regulations)), speciation-preserving testing methods (linked to [testing](/testing)), microbiome effects (linked to [microbiome](/microbiome)), vulnerable populations, open questions, and sources.

## Chromium research: qualified source context

One Nilore, Islamabad soil sample characterization reported bulk chromium of 28.15 µg/g, with wet/dry basis unstated (Table 2). The source subsequently included that native total-chromium value in a calculation labelled Cr(VI) removal; its oxidation state was not established. [[sources/khalid2008-islamabad-chromium-electrokinetic-remediation]]

A chromium special-issue editorial provides secondary environmental and remediation context. Its crustal-background comparator is not a measured site concentration or legal limit, and its summarized fish values require the underlying study before primary occurrence reuse (p1780). [[sources/saha2022-chromium-sources-remediation-editorial]]

## Nahavand farm study: evidence limitations

A Nahavand Plain farm study measured total chromium by ICP-AES in environmental and whole-biomass samples. Chromium was not speciated, so subsequent risk-model inputs cannot turn the occurrence measurements into Cr(VI) data. Whole aerial biomass also cannot substitute for isolated edible grain or root tissue (Methods pp4–5; Tables 6–9). [[sources/sharafi2026-nahavand-soil-water-crop-metals]]

## Arzew surface-soil survey

Arzew industrial soils had chromium mean 51.17 mg/kg, median 45.36 mg/kg and range 11.33–193.36 mg/kg (N=14). The aqua-regia/ICP-AES method did not resolve oxidation state. Source risk calculations cannot convert these elemental results into Cr(VI) measurements (Boudia et al. 2019, Table 2; Methods 2.3). [[sources/authors2019-spatial-contamination-and-health-risks-of-heavy-metal]]

## China imported pet foods: preprint evidence

Du et al.’s 2024 preprint reports chromium in all 93 pet-food products, mean 3.47 mg/kg and range 0.48–15.47 mg/kg (Table 3). The assay did not speciate chromium. Source-modeled hazard indices cannot turn those results into measuredCr(VI), and the later publication represents the same sample cohort. [[sources/cat2024-individual-and-combined-contamination-of-the-heavy-metals]]

## Texas animal-feed survey

Texas mineral feed products had unspeciated chromium mean 40.43 mg/kg, SD 87.48 and median 9.3 (N=124; Dai et al. 2016, Table 3). Beef-cattle feed had mean 4.91 mg/kg and SD 3.92 (N=160). These heterogeneous feed categories and zero-substituted summaries do not measure Cr(VI), human-food concentrations or clinical outcomes. [[sources/dai2016-texas-animal-feed-metals]]

## Ganga freshwater-fish study: qualified evidence

Maurya et al. measured unspeciated chromium in Ganga water and freshwater-fish tissues. River-water means were 0.32 mg/L at Kanpur, 0.85 at Allahabad, 0.36 at Mirzapur and 0.45 at Varanasi (2019, Table 3). Fish-table basis and exposure-model units are internally inconsistent. None of these measurements identifies Cr(VI) or validates a species-specific health-risk estimate. [[sources/authors2019-toxicology-reports-6-2019-472-481]]

<!-- BEGIN: hmi-metal-faq -->
## Frequently asked questions
<!-- audience: consumer, educator -->

### What is the difference between trivalent and hexavalent chromium in food?

Chromium in the food system is a split case between two species. Trivalent chromium (Cr3+) has an ambiguous history as a putative essential trace element and is the form found in most dietary supplements. Hexavalent chromium (Cr6+) is a carcinogen by inhalation and has documented toxicity by ingestion, including in drinking water, so the two species differ sharply and are tracked separately.

### How does hexavalent chromium get into food?

According to the page, hexavalent chromium's relevance to food is principally through drinking water used in food preparation, through meat processing with certain contact materials, and through supply-chain contamination. Because of these distinct pathways, Cr6+ is handled on its own dedicated page separate from trivalent chromium.

### What health effects are linked to hexavalent chromium exposure?

Drawing on [[sources/ufelle2021-metals-chapter|Ufelle & Barchowsky 2021]], hexavalent chromium is absorbed through sulfate and phosphate transporters and is associated with corrosive injury, allergic contact dermatitis, and acute renal injury after high-dose ingestion. That source also frames Cr(VI) in terms of lung cancer and genotoxicity. These effects are specific to the hexavalent species.

### Is trivalent chromium (the kind in supplements) considered safe or essential?

The page describes trivalent chromium as having an ambiguous history as a putative essential trace element, and notes it is the species present in most dietary supplements. It does not resolve the essentiality question; the page is a sourced stub and states that Cr-III-specific expansion, including the essentiality debate and supplement context, awaits dedicated corpus ingest.

### Why does it matter whether a study reports 'total chromium' instead of the specific species?

The wiki maintains the Cr3+ versus Cr6+ distinction wherever the source permits, and flags ambiguity when a study reports total chromium without specifying the species. Sources that report total chromium without speciating are treated only as total-chromium occurrence context and must not be silently substituted for hexavalent chromium, because Cr-VI's toxicology, occurrence, regulatory, and testing posture all differ sharply from Cr-III.
<!-- END: hmi-metal-faq -->

## Update history

The five most recent substantive edits to this page, classified major (evidence or structure moved), correction (a published value or statement was wrong and has been fixed), or minor (narrative rewritten without changing the underlying evidence). Each description is derived from what the edit did to this page; the linked commit is the authoritative record, routine regeneration passes are excluded, and the full version history lives in [git](https://github.com/paleofoundation/heavymetalindex).

## Additional geographic and remediation evidence

**[[sources/abubakar2025-faisalabad-sewage-vegetables|Muhammad Abubakar, 2025]].** Abubakar and colleagues measured seven metals in sewage water, soil, spinach and cauliflower at two peri-urban sites in Faisalabad, Pakistan. The paper reports substantial differences between the two crop/site combinations, but its design does not separate plant-species effects from location. Concentrations are retained with the source’s measurement and tissue limitations. These are localized paired environmental and crop measurements, with plant results routed as leafy-tissue evidence. They do not establish a cauliflower-head concentration, a fresh-weight concentration, or a causal reduction in biomass from metal exposure. Industrial source attribution is proposed by the authors, rather than measured by a source-apportionment experiment.

**[[sources/joseph2025-gobbiya-dam-vegetable-metals|John Joseph, 2025]].** Joseph and colleagues measured seven metals in kenaf, chili pepper, tomato and spinach plant parts, with associated soil and irrigation water from Gobbiya Dam in Nigeria. The paper separates edible leaves or fruits from roots and stems. Its concentration tables are preserved independently of several inconsistent safety comparisons and a soil-index calculation that uses water standards. The organ-resolved dry-weight values support local occurrence and irrigation-pathway analysis. Roots, stems and non-edible leaves are retained as pathway context rather than assigned to edible-product distributions. Broad leafy-vegetable and herb/spice ingredient routes retain kenaf and chili identity in the structured record without substituting a named cultivar.

**[[sources/dyakova2023-voronezh-medicinal-plant-metals|Nina Alekseevna Dyakova, 2023]].** Dyakova compares nine elements in medicinal plant materials collected in disturbed and control environments in Russia’s Voronezh region during 2015–2020. The reported ranges preserve tissue and environmental setting; the paper also proposes regional background means across species. Supports geographic and tissue-specific contamination evidence for botanical raw materials and environmental attribution hypotheses. It does not measure finished supplements or isolate an individual polluter’s causal contribution.

**[[sources/sumalatha2019-bangalore-sludge-washing|J. Sumalatha, 2019]].** Column washing of Bangalore-area industrial sludge removed substantially more metals with hydrochloric acid plus EDTA than with distilled water. This is a remediation experiment relevant to sludge contamination pathways, with no measurements in food or a demonstrated safe land-application endpoint. Supports laboratory washing and contaminant-transport comparisons. Metal-rich leachate still requires management; the study does not establish agricultural reuse or field-scale safety.

**[[sources/zandi2023-rice-root-chromium-speciation|Peiman Zandi, 2023]].** Rice seedlings exposed to trivalent chromium retained chromium in roots and surface iron plaques. Synchrotron speciation distinguished ferrihydrite-bound from organic-associated Cr(III), with different proportions in root tips and mature zones; the study concerns an upstream uptake mechanism rather than grain contamination. Supports chromium uptake and iron-plaque pathway evidence. Hotspot spectral fractions are not bulk rice-grain species fractions and cannot substitute for a Cr(VI) food result. Release of plaque-bound Cr during dissolution qualifies any assumption of permanent sequestration.

**[[sources/kananke2026-sri-lanka-leaf-bioaccessibility|Thilini Kananke, 2026]].** This Sri Lankan study compares elemental concentrations and simulated digestive release in two leafy vegetables across five growing areas. Raw, coconut-milk-cooked and coconut-oil-stir-fried portions were tested separately; lower in-vitro release does not establish lower human absorption or safe consumption. Supports region-specific leafy-vegetable occurrence and cooking/bioaccessibility comparisons. Processing effects are not uniform: for example Wellampitiya A. sessilis gastric Pb is 15.3% raw and 20.7% cooked. Coconut additions can change the matrix; an in-vitro endpoint is not in-vivo absorption.

**[[sources/enogbe2025-bwari-chocolate-metals|Emmanuel Joy Enogbe, 2025]].** Two anonymous chocolate products from Bwari market contained measurable nickel, iron, copper, chromium and lead. Their reported sample concentrations are preserved independently of inconsistent dispersion, intake means and safety comparisons in the paper. Provides a small local occurrence observation with anonymous products. It cannot establish market prevalence, source attribution, or a no-risk conclusion. Total Cr is not Cr(VI).

**[[sources/bashkin2021-vegetable-risk-review|Vladimir N. Bashkin, 2021]].** Bashkin and Galiulina review vegetable-associated metal exposure assessment and microbial/agronomic risk-management approaches. Chinese and Russian case-study values are secondary reports; they remain traceable to the cited studies and are not counted as new independent occurrence samples. Supports exposure-method and remediation literature discovery. Bacterial effects can increase as well as decrease uptake depending on organism, metal and plant; the review does not establish a universal inoculant intervention. Secondary model outputs are not contemporary health or certification thresholds.
